### [Honey, I shrunk the brain](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/honey-i-shrunk-the-brain)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 7, 2026 12:01 AM GMT<br /><br /><p>When cryoprotectants are perfused through the blood vessels in the brain, they cannot cross the blood-brain barrier as fast as water can move in the opposite direction. And cryoprotectants generally have a much higher osmotic concentration than the typical blood plasma. For example, the cryoprotectant solution M22 has an osmotic concentration around 100 times higher.</p><p>As a result, in a successful cryoprotectant perfusion (without fixatives), water rushes out of the tissue into the blood vessels, the tissue dehydrates, and you end up with a shrunken brain that is visibly pulled away from the skull. The brain weight goes down by 50% or more. This is currently considered a good sign of cryoprotectant perfusion quality.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/h7qplcluzqd6rjcfgdec" /><figcaption><a href="http://youtube.com/watch?v=0ciz3UpA6Rc"><u>case report A-1002 is an example of a shrunken brain</u></a></figcaption></figure><p>Far be it from me to say that a brain preservation method will not work because it seems weird. I myself have proposed that aldehyde fixation — something which is definitively lethal by contemporary medical criteria — <a href="https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/medical-technology/articles/10.3389/fmedt.2024.1400615/full"><u>may allow people to be revived with meaningful memories intact if humanity develops sufficiently advanced technology in the future</u></a>. So I’m not going to use the absurdity heuristic here.</p><p>Instead, the key question is what this severe dehydration does to the nanometer-scale structures in the brain, such as the connections between neurons, that are thought to be the key parts of the information that encodes long-term memories.</p><p>Previous attempts at imaging this type of brain tissue were stymied <a href="https://www.brainpreservation.org/21cm-cryopreservation-eval-page/"><u>because the severe dehydration made the tissue look unrecognizable</u></a>. Synapses could be seen, but it wasn’t possible to clearly identify individual neurites or trace them to see whether the connectome is intact:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/cwkekoxq5ynceaiwwmcd" /><figcaption>https://www.brainpreservation.org/21cm-cryopreservation-eval-page/</figcaption></figure><p><a href="https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf"><u>A new paper from Greg Fahy et al at 21st Century Medicine</u></a> provides the most detailed look yet at what happens to brain ultrastructure during vitrification. So naturally, I had a look at it.</p><p><strong>What do they think of non-vitrification approaches?</strong></p><p>First, the paper opens with a very interesting review of prior work on brain cryopreservation, including some original data from <a href="https://neurobiology.substack.com/p/can-we-freeze-time"><u>the ever-controversial experiments of Isamu Suda performed in the 1960s</u></a>.</p><p>The authors are not very enthused about freezing-based approaches. They show electron micrographs of rabbit brains that were perfused with glycerol and slowly frozen, which have large ice cavities that grossly distort the tissue:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/h4pph02ievfiouvs5nyr" /><figcaption>https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf</figcaption></figure><p>Their position seems to be that vitrification — i.e., cooling without ice formation — is the only serious path forward for brain cryopreservation. But vitrification requires especially high concentrations of cryoprotectants, which causes the severe dehydration that has made it difficult to assess whether the connectome is preserved.</p><p><strong>Rabbit experiments</strong></p><p>They performed two types of rabbit experiments.</p><p>In the first group, rabbit brains were perfused with M22 for 60 minutes, vitrified, rewarmed, and then fixed with a solution that still contains the same concentration of cryoprotectant. This shows us what the tissue looks like in its shrunken state. At high magnification they can identify synapses, mitochondria, and what appears to be some morphologically intact cell membranes. But everything is compressed, making it difficult to clearly distinguish or trace individual cellular processes. These look similar to the previous images of vitrified brain tissue.</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 89.17%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/zlt5lb8fcayvlkveilax" /><figcaption>https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf</figcaption></figure><p>In the second group, rabbit brains were perfused with M22 and then gradually diluted back to a lower concentration of cryoprotectant before fixation — to 5M, 3M, or 1M. This tests the extent to which the shrinkage is reversible.</p><p>Although notably, it seems to me that most of the shrinkage is expected to occur between 0 and ~3M concentration of cryoprotectant. So my understanding is that diluting from full M22 back to 3M or 5M wouldn’t be expected to reverse most of the dehydration, although I might be wrong about that:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/uokesfcpcq0sr2wmjinm" /><figcaption>https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf</figcaption></figure><p>Anyway, they found that at 5M, the tissue is still quite shrunken. After reversal to 3M, though, things look considerably better, as the neurons have more normal-looking apical axons, and synapses with visible presynaptic vesicles can be identified. This is probably their best-looking EM data. However, there are various forms of damage, such as the wavy intracellular white spaces, that I don’t understand the cause of. Also, there are some places in the zoomed-in image (C) where I can’t really tell whether I am looking at two smaller processes or one larger one:</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 59.2%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/yn3m6sfr1xkvzz8jojje" /><figcaption>https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf</figcaption></figure><p>When they reverse to 1M (Fig 10), things go a bit wrong. While they can see nicely preserved synapses with clear presynaptic vesicles, they also see what they call “exploded” neurons, which they attribute to osmotic damage from removing the cryoprotectant too quickly.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/manoy19oujqwkuigdyvb" /><figcaption>https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf</figcaption></figure><p>They report that this problem is solvable, by using osmolytes to counterbalance the intracellular cryoprotectant during washout. They report that this can prevent this ultrastructural damage “even when all M22 is washed out of the brain.” But this data is not presented in the paper. It’s cited as “Spindler et al., in preparation.”</p><p><strong>Human data</strong></p><p>The human data comes from a single brain, that of a 73-year-old terminal cancer patient who donated biopsy samples of his brain for this research. His brain had significant ischemic injury before preservation even began, consisting of two days of agonal hypoxia before legal death, then three hours of cold ischemia before perfusion started. That’s important because it’s actually an example of realistic brain preservation conditions.</p><p>Cortical biopsies were taken after whole-brain M22 perfusion, plunged into liquid nitrogen, and stored for four years. They were then warmed and processed in different ways.</p><p>Some biopsies were warmed straight into fixative containing M22, showing us the fully dehydrated state. As expected, it is severely shrunken and electron-dense, but without obvious ice damage. On electron microscopy, they can identify synapses and some intact-appearing membranes.</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 47.69%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/hjhrm8fvhcsblepwromf" /><figcaption>https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf</figcaption></figure><p>Other biopsies of the human brain were rewarmed into diluted M22 (75% or 66%) before fixation. Using light microscopy, they report that this partial rehydration caused cells to regain their general characteristic shapes, with neurites visible.</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 50.35%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/m83emku71gdpjpgj0dsa" /><figcaption>https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.01.28.702375v1.full.pdf</figcaption></figure><p>However, the rehydrated human tissue was only examined by light microscopy, not electron microscopy.</p><p><a href="https://www.brainpreservation.org/21cm-cryopreservation-eval-page/"><u>One of the main concerns with vitrification has long been</u></a> that the severe dehydration might be masking damage to the structure of the brain. For example, if the cell membranes are broken apart or there are areas of washed out structure due to ischemia and rapid osmotic shifts during perfusion, we might not be able to see it when everything is compressed together.</p><p>Because the electron microscopy that they did show from the human brain tissue is still so compacted, we can’t really evaluate for the presence or absence of such damage yet with much certainty.</p><p><strong>Summary</strong></p><p>In brain preservation, as much as possible, we need objective metrics. One metric that has been proposed by <a href="https://www.brainpreservation.org/tech-prize/"><u>Ken Hayworth</u></a> and <a href="https://www.amazon.com/Connectome-How-Brains-Wiring-Makes/dp/0547678592"><u>Sebastian Seung</u></a> is to see whether it is possible to trace the connectome of a preserved brain. <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2507.17274v2"><u>This is widely viewed by researchers as one of the best available metrics of preservation quality</u></a>.</p><p>In ideal laboratory animal settings (rabbits), the key result is that they show partial reversibility (to 3M) with improved electron microscopy quality, but still without enough reversibility to see clearly traceable processes across the 2d image. They report that complete washout is actually possible with a new method that they developed, which is fantastic news. But this rests on unpublished work that we will have to wait to see in the future.</p><p>Ideally, they would show in this future work that they can reliably trace the connectome across randomly sampled areas of the brain, which would allow the pure vitrification approach to reach parity with aldehyde-based methods in ideal laboratory animal settings, and shift the debate to which method is the best at structural preservation in realistic settings.</p><p>In the single human case, they show that perfusion-based vitrification is feasible in at least some parts of the brain even hours after legal death, and that cells regain their general characteristic shapes after partial rehydration. But the rehydrated human tissue was only examined by light microscopy, not electron microscopy, so we can’t tell whether the connectome is likely to be traceable in the local area where this biopsy sample was taken from.</p><p>This paper is clearly a step forward and a very important contribution to the brain preservation literature. I would like to personally thank the authors for their important work, and also for explaining how this type of method could potentially be used for medical time travel, which is a premise I totally agree with. Not surprisingly for a single paper, it alone has not resolved the key uncertainties about vitrification-based brain preservation.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KvbBYaKmGcJKvvWd8/honey-i-shrunk-the-brain#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Parks Aren't Nature](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/parks-aren-t-nature)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 6, 2026 6:27 PM GMT<br /><br /><h1><strong>I.</strong></h1><p>I love dogs.</p><p>I grew up in a two-dog household, and my future plans have always included at least one dog. When I pass a dog on the street, I often point and exclaim “Puppy!”, no matter how inappropriate it is for a grown man to do so, because all dogs are puppies and all puppies are adorable and I need everyone to know this.</p><p>Why do I love dogs?</p><p>They’re loyal and loving and giving, and even though they bark at passing cars and occasionally pee on the carpet having them in my life makes it unquestionably better.</p><p>The thing is, dogs as they exist today are a lot of things, but they aren’t <i>natural</i>.</p><p>Nature didn’t shape dogs, didn’t produce the breeds we see every day. It wasn’t like Darwin went to an island and found that a species of wolf had been separated by a mountain chain and on one side were Golden Retrievers and the other Yorkshire Terriers.</p><p>Dogs exist today as the result of millennia of co-adaptation and selective breeding by humans. They’re animals, yes, and Nature technically made the base form, but we humans molded them into shapes more compatible with us. Most dogs are very safe to have around humans.</p><p>But there is an animal that is a more <i>natural</i> Canid: <i>Wolves.</i></p><p>And wolves are a lot of things, but they’re not pets. They aren’t domesticated; they aren’t bred for cuddliness and kisses. A wolf will hurt and kill and eat you.</p><p>Wolves are wild animals in their state of nature, <a href="https://www.tapestryinstitute.org/tornado/tennyson.html">red in tooth and claw</a>.</p><p>The thing is, this distinction between dogs and wolves - between nature <i>tamed </i>and nature <i>wild</i> - this matters, when we think about who we humans are and what we want the world around us to look like. We might say we enjoy the <i>natural </i>world, might want less deforestation and more green spaces, but I’ve yet to meet anyone who wants <i>actual wolves</i> running around their neighborhood. We might go to farm-to-table restaurants and only eat organic, free-range eggs, but chickens <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feral_chicken">mostly</a> don’t exist in the wild for good reason.</p><p>In a first-world country, or even in any populous city, almost everyone’s experience of what we call ‘nature’ is that of <i>dogs</i>, not <i>wolves</i>. Nature <i>tamed</i>, not Nature <i>wild. </i>And so I think it pays to be precise what it means when we say <i>nature</i>, because it’s not as simple as ‘non-human animal’ or ‘uninhabited area’.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="Wolf - Wikipedia" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/xofc82yzmjjzlwafi1bc" /><figcaption>A wolf, red in tooth and claw.</figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="Chihuahua Dog Breed Information" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/mjnfmlylnyowhbxwxrwf" /><figcaption>A Chihuahua, evidence of selective breeding gone horribly wrong.</figcaption></figure><h1><strong>II.</strong></h1><p>There’s something called an <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeal_to_nature"><i>appeal to nature</i></a>, which is apparently distinct from the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalistic_fallacy">naturalistic fallacy</a>, because naming things clearly is not a strength of philosophy.</p><p>Anyway, an <i>appeal to nature</i> is the idea that <i>natural</i> equates to <i>good</i>. It’s behind all the marketing in a grocery store that advertises organic, non-GMO, free-range, grass-fed, <a href="https://xkcd.com/641/">asbestos-free</a> Doritos.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="Free" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/deyy56vfzvfgg3ntuc3i" /><figcaption>Captioning an XKCD is kind of putting a hat on a hat, but this is how I feel when I see <a href="https://www.target.com/c/wine-beer-liquor-grocery/gluten-free/-/N-5xsxvZb8gba"><u>gluten-free wine</u></a>. Gluten comes from grains like wheat. How does <i>wheat</i> get in your wine?</figcaption></figure><p>Once you point it out, the idea that something is axiomatically good just because it’s <i>natural</i> is kind of silly; after all, cockroaches are perfectly natural, as is gangrene, athlete’s foot, and <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kIGcosb22_Y">Donald Trump’s hair</a>. But most people have a tendency to buy into this just a little. After all, isn’t real food with real names better for you than Butylated Hydroxytoluene or Red Dye #5?</p><p>There’s multiple problems with an appeal to nature - for one, vaccines are pretty unnatural, but so is not dying of tetanus - but the one I’d like to focus on is the idea that <i>natural</i> is a quality something either has or it doesn’t.</p><p>I think a lot of people think about whether something is natural or not like this:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/szngbm66attmac1palnx" /></figure><p>But the truth, like many things, is not so simple. Things, especially what we think of as ‘the natural world’, are more like this:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/sbmrbxtm3c3mqvvkbady" /></figure><p>In its own way, crop-covered farmland is no more ‘natural’ than the concrete jungle of New York City, even though the former is made of plants and the latter of stone and steel and glass. Both are curated by humanity, just for different goals.</p><h1><strong>III.</strong></h1><p>What was the natural world like, before humans befouled it? What was paradise, before we <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tvtJPs8IDgU">paved it and put up a parking lot</a>?</p><p>What was a person’s experience of nature, back before it was tamed?</p><p>Nature was <i>terrible</i>. And not in a sarcastic, that-movie-was-terrible kind of way, but in that it genuinely inspired <i>terror</i>. Nature was the domain of the uncertain, the cataclysmic, the cruel and uncaring world from which existence had to be torn day in and day out.</p><p>A farmer’s experience of nature would have been a constant battle to raise crops, hoping and praying that there would be enough rain to water them but not enough to wash them away, that locusts or other insects wouldn’t eat or foul them, that disease and fungus wouldn’t rot crops from the inside out. The ancient farmer was always only a few strokes of bad luck from starvation, and nature was the lottery they played every day of their lives.</p><p>Compare this to the farmer of today, who ensures their crops get enough water no matter what via massive irrigation, who uses pesticides to annihilate pests, who presides over massive machinery as it trundles along seeding and harvesting their crops. The farmer of today has access to genetically modified strains of plants that resist disease and grow larger with more yield than any ancient farmer could have hoped to have.</p><p>Is the ancient farmer in some sense doing something more <i>natural</i>? Sure, if by <i>natural</i> you mean they’re operating closer to the state of the pre-human natural world. Does that mean that what modern farmers do is <i>unnatural</i>?</p><p>I don’t think so.</p><p>Farmers have tamed nature, and this is <i>good</i>. This gives us abundant cheap food, enough to feed everyone on earth while only a tiny percentage of the population is needed to produce it.</p><p><i>(The fact that people still go hungry and starve is an issue of distribution, not production. We </i><a href="https://ourworldindata.org/food-supply"><i>make enough calories</i></a><i> to feed everyone.)</i></p><p>And this contrast between <i>more natural</i> and <i>less natural</i> on the spectrum, what I called <i>nature wild</i> and <i>nature tamed</i> above, is <i>everywhere</i>.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="Corn | History, Cultivation, Uses, &amp; Description | Britannica" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/lyavqttqpolitodpf8mn" /><figcaption>Modern corn. This is the stuff High Fructose Corn Syrup comes from!</figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/mhbwy6wx3il51o13itvv" /><figcaption>The <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize"><u>Teosintes</u></a> from which modern corn was bred. Which would you rather grow?</figcaption></figure><h1><strong>IV.</strong></h1><p>At this point, I’ve hopefully convinced you of the title of the post. A park isn’t really <i>natural</i>, any more than a Chihuahua is a wolf. It’s something sculpted, pruned, weeded, and landscaped. It’s full of plants, sure, but it’s an example of <i>nature tamed</i>, not <i>nature wild</i>.</p><p>How about going on a hike? That’s nature, right?</p><p>Not really.</p><p>Even if you’re hiking through a national park or other untouched terrain, even if you’re setting foot somewhere with wolves and bears and poison ivy where no human has ever ventured, simply by virtue of existing in the 21st century you’re still experiencing something very different than what our ancestors would have, long ago.</p><p>Today we have satellites overhead and GPS to Globally Position us wherever we are, and weather simulations to tell us what to expect the sky to do. We have rugged clothes that can be cheaply replaced if torn, and mass-produced boots with rubber soles that won’t get pierced by thorns or rocks. We have plastic and metal bottles to store water and abundant food to pack for ourselves. We have thermal sleeping bags and bug spray and sunscreen and sunglasses to keep us comfortable. We have first-aid kits with antibiotics and alcohol swabs and itch creams and sterile bandages.</p><p>Our distant ancestors had none of those.</p><p>What would venturing into the wilds have been like to our distant ancestors?</p><p>They knew of some of the dangers they’d face: Inclement weather, wild animals, getting lost and having no way to contact help or navigate back to the group. But there were other dangers that they must have realized, even if they didn’t know the causes: infection, disease, rot. A single cut gone untreated, a mild scrape gotten while pushing aside a thorny plant, and gangrene could set in.</p><p>Going into nature meant risking your life, even if it might not have felt that way at the time. Sure, untouched woods might be beautiful, but nature is often at its most beautiful when it’s at its most deadly. <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aposematism">Bright colors usually mean poison</a> in the natural world.</p><p>Consider also the perils of simple exposure: a cool night can spell death for someone without shelter or proper clothes or a fire. Add rain and wind, and anyone venturing beyond human settlements had to be wary of dying soaked and cold.</p><h1><strong>V.</strong></h1><p>There are places, in our world, that are still <i>natural</i>. Untamed.</p><p>The Amazon Rainforest.</p><p>The Australian Outback.</p><p>And going into those places, unprepared, without a guide, is quite likely to get you killed.</p><p><i>That</i> is about as natural as it gets, as natural as the vacuum of space, and only slightly more hospitable. That our ancestors were able to survive in such environments - that there are people today who can live in such environments - is amazing, but it comes with a high cost.</p><p>People who have to fight nature every day to survive are doing just that - surviving. They can’t relax with a good book or take a hot shower. They can’t get into long debates about trivial things with their friends over drinks, or have a poker night once a week. They can’t take vacations or paid sick days, and the only insurance available to them is the beneficence of their community. There is no <i>retirement</i>, for them; if they stop struggling to survive, they stop surviving.</p><p>More fundamentally, constantly struggling to survive takes its toll on a person’s body and mind. Constant stress ages you, wears you down, leaves you ragged and weary and unable to relax.</p><p>There’s a lot of nostalgia for the past, but I think people consistently underestimate just how <i>hard</i> life was for those who came before us. How much they had to struggle against the world just to keep living. How much they suffered.</p><p>Is the world we humans have built for ourselves less <i>natural</i> than it used to be?</p><p>Of course.</p><p>It’s also far more forgiving, far more comfortable, and <a href="https://ourworldindata.org/child-mortality">far less tragic</a>.</p><h1><strong>VI.</strong></h1><p>Appeals to nature argue that <i>natural </i>means <i>better</i>.</p><p>This appeal is a fallacy because it’s wrong, but it’s wrong in two ways.</p><p>The first is simple: <i>artificial</i> does not equate to <i>worse</i>. Plastic is far superior to the materials humans used before it; purified metals and alloys are better than ores; our sewer and drinking water systems are far better for us than drinking ‘natural’, unfiltered water.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="What are your anime words of wisdom? : r/animequestions" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/ps5miyhjahugvdoykdfd" /><figcaption>There’s no law that says what’s artificial can’t surpass what’s natural.</figcaption></figure><p>The second is that what we, in a 21st century first-world country, think of as <i>nature</i> is a tamed thing, something pruned and weeded and cultivated, and ultimately no more <i>natural</i> than a suburban lawn.</p><p>In other words, appeals to nature are always dependent on the reference frame they’re made from. If you’re standing in the middle of New York city and yearning for nature, you’re probably yearning for pine trees and dandelions and fireflies, not <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchineel">trees of death</a> and poison ivy and malaria-carrying mosquitoes, even though the latter are just as <i>natural</i> as the former.</p><p>What we think of as ‘nature’ has already been massively affected by humanity over the centuries. Even the moon now has human footprints and a human flag on it:</p><figure class="image"><img alt="U.S. Flag on the Moon" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/viluibcensyqhp1aycyv" /><figcaption>The species <i>flagus usa-us</i> can be found sprouting up from several celestial bodies. It’s considered by some to be an invasive species, by others a hardy and welcome addition to the ecosystem…</figcaption></figure><p>Nature, to most Americans, is something safe and peaceful and beautiful. It’s sitting on your porch watching a sunset, or seeing autumn plumage on the trees, or sitting around a campfire with your friends. We tend to only think of it as horrifying and destructive during severe weather events and natural disasters (which, as actual climate change scientists will tell you, are still quite <i>natural</i>; plenty of them happened before we humans dumped a bunch of carbon in the atmosphere, and plenty will happen after).</p><p>In other words, appeals to nature are wrong because we’re wrong about what nature is actually <i>like</i>. It has always been beautiful, but only as humanity shaped it has it become good<i> for us.</i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa1hrnt4cb4a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna1hrnt4cb4a">[1]</a></sup></span></p><h1><strong>VII.</strong></h1><p>If you look at the human experience of nature over history, what you see is humans shaping and crafting their environments to be more and more friendly to them, until the default first-world conception of <i>nature</i> is something lovely and harmless, rather than the murderous (if beautiful) thing it once was.</p><p>And while the full argument is beyond the scope of this post, I think this is a good thing.</p><p>Are there things lost, as nature is tamed? Yes.</p><p>Wolves are beautiful, elegant creatures. Chihuahuas are not.</p><p>But I’d much rather have a Chihuahua<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrwu2qzbicul"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrwu2qzbicul">[2]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;as a pet than a wolf.</p><p>I’m not telling you not to enjoy going outside; just that, next time you go to the park or take a hike, understand that unless you’re trekking through the Amazon or the Australian Outback, your experience is more like that of eating a modern GMO fruit than anything our ancestors might have had: easier, safer, and altogether more delicious.</p><p>So maybe, the next time you’re taking a walk outside your climate-controlled residence to get some fresh air, take a second to appreciate the ‘less natural’ nature around you, and the benefits of living in a world so much more adapted to humanity than it used to be.</p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fna1hrnt4cb4a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa1hrnt4cb4a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Some argue that nature is good <i>qua</i> nature, as in, a fundamental good by itself. I’m not one of them. My circle of concern extends to sapient beings of all kinds, and somewhat to some kinds of animals, but I don’t consider plants, fungi, or bacteria to have any intrinsic moral worth.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrwu2qzbicul"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrwu2qzbicul">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Actually a Shih Tzu, though I think the point stands.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cjxPFxAe5WRKA6SeF/parks-aren-t-nature#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Claude Code #4: From The Before Times](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iwX2aJPKtyKAbLdip/claude-code-4-from-the-before-times)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Published on February 6, 2026 6:01 PM GMT<br /><br /><p>Claude Opus 4.6 and agent swarms were announced yesterday. That’s some big upgrades for Claude Code.</p>
<p>OpenAI, the competition, offered us GPT-5.3-Codex, and this week gave us an app form of Codex that already has a million active users.</p>
<p>That’s all very exciting, and next week is going to be about covering that.</p>
<p>This post is about all the cool things that happened before that, which we will be building upon now that capabilities have further advanced. This if from Before Times.</p>
<p>Almost all of it still applies. I haven’t had much chance yet to work with Opus 4.6, but as far as I can tell you should mostly keep on doing what you were doing before that switch, only everything will work better. Maybe get a bit more ambitious. Agent swarms might be more of a technique shifter, but we need to give that some time.</p>

<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/185474971/the-lighter-side">The Lighter Side.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>Claude Code and Cowork Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ns123abc/status/2013955320318427207">Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang offered Claude a huge endorsement on January 21</a>, calling it incredible and saying every software company needs to use it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/emollick/status/2015512532056764490">Ethan Mollick</a>: This game was 100% designed, tested, and made by Claude Code with the instructions to “make a complete Sierra-style adventure game with EGA-like graphics and text parser, with 10-15 minutes of gameplay.” I then told it to playtest the game &amp; deploy.</p>
<p>Play: <a href="https://enchanted-lighthouse-game.netlify.app">https://enchanted-lighthouse-game.netlify.app</a></p>
<p>It was a single prompt for the entire game, and then a prompt to playtest and improve the outcome.</p>
<p>I gave it an agent that can connect to GPT image gen.</p>

<div></div>
</div>

</blockquote>
<p>Iterative image generation sounds pretty cool:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/omarsar0/status/2017296558169952517">elvis</a>: I just used the new Claude Code Playground plugin to level up my Nano Banana Image generator skill.</p>
<p>My skill has a self-improving loop, but with the playground skill, I can also pass precise annotations to nano banana as it improves the images.</p>
<p>I have built a Skill for Claude Code that leverages the nano banana image generation model via API.</p>
<p>I built it like that because I have had a lot of success generating images with nano banana in an agentic self-improving loop. It can dynamically make API requests and improve images really well.</p>
<p>With the Playground plugin, I can take it one step further. I can now provide precise annotations that the agentic loop can leverage to make more optimal API calls in the hope of improving the images further. Visual cues are extremely powerful for agents, and this is a sort of proxy for that.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2017759936919572482">Ado cancels all his Amazon Subscribe and Save orders</a> via Claude for Chrome. Sameer points out that <a href="https://t.co/K971ZUIMrW">a Chrome Extension can do this more efficiently and have a better UI</a>, but there is great joy in not having to choose and install a new tool to do a new thing. Yes, if you were doing this a lot you’d use a Chrome Extension, or have Claude Code build you a new one to your taste.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/2018043254986703167">I agree with Andrej Karpathy, you should use RSS feeds</a> wherever feasible to guard your information flow. I use Feedly, he suggests NetNewsWire or vibe coding your own reader. It is unfortunate that Twitter does not play nice with such a setup.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sethlazar/status/2018086494125318535">Seth Lazar</a>: I wrote about the idea of building an “Attention Guardian” agent back in 2023. Genuinely think it’s feasible now. Claude Code is now building up a workflow to go across all these different sources, with a long description of what I’m interested in, and create a new feed.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/notnotstorm/status/2018037481409855594">Storm points out that anything you can do with a terminal interface</a> you can in theory do better with a graphical interface (GUI), but the people building GUIs don’t give you the things you want: Information density, low latency, no ads, shortcuts, open source, composable, tileable, scriptable. It’s just that no one does it.</p>


<h4>The Efficient Market Hypothesis Is False</h4>


<p>What the market has instead is a sense of humor.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/modestproposal1/status/2018792278643650701">modest proposal</a>: March 12, 2020 was the trading day after Tom Hanks said he had covid and the NBA shut down, Expedia fell 15.2% and BKNG fell 11.2%.</p>
<p>February 3, 2026 which was the day Claude Code legal connector was announced, Expedia fell 15.3% and BKNG fell 9.4%.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2019146895705403642">Then software drove itself off a cliff generally</a> (y-axis goes from .012 to .018), and then after this graph was posted it kept going, all supposedly in response to information that, from where I sit, was rather old news the whole time.</p>

This seems like the first huge shift in market behavior I’ve seen caused by AI capabilities. Why wasn’t this all over the TL?</p>

<p>Okay, so yeah, combined with what has happened since then that’s DeepSeek 2.0, a large move down on entirely expected news.</p>
<p>Should software have already been lower? That’s a reasonable position, but there’s no way that <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-02-03/legal-software-stocks-plunge-as-anthropic-releases-new-ai-tool?embedded-checkout=true">it should have dropped this much in response to this news</a>. If you declared <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-02-03/-get-me-out-traders-dump-software-stocks-as-ai-fears-take-hold">SaaSpocalypse on February 3</a> you should have done so a month ago. Alas, no, I did not trade on this, because it’s not obvious to me we should be SaaSpocalypsing at all and it wasn’t obvious this wasn’t priced in.</p>
<p>Now we are in a period where all the tech stocks are moving around violently, usually in full wrong way moves. I continue not to trade on any of it. I do have some ammo, but I also am already plenty long and have been for a while, so I’m not going to fire unless I see the whites of their eyes.</p>


<h4>Inflection Point</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2015979257038831967">Andrej Karpathy updates us</a> that he was one of many who went from 80% manual coding and autocomplete in November to 80% agentic coding in December. Whole thing is worth reading.</p>
<blockquote><p>Andrej Karpathy: This is easily the biggest change to my basic coding workflow in ~2 decades of programming and it happened over the course of a few weeks. I’d expect something similar to be happening to well into double digit percent of engineers out there, while the awareness of it in the general population feels well into low single digit percent.</p></blockquote>
<p>He’s still behind the curve, I’m with Boris Cherny at 100% agentic coding. Then again, excluding quotes I’m still at almost 100% manual writing for posts.</p>
<blockquote><p><strong>IDEs/agent swarms/fallability</strong>. Both the “no need for IDE anymore” hype and the “agent swarm” hype is imo too much for right now. The models definitely still make mistakes and if you have any code you actually care about I would watch them like a hawk, in a nice large IDE on the side. The mistakes have changed a lot – they are not simple syntax errors anymore, they are subtle conceptual errors that a slightly sloppy, hasty junior dev might do.​</p>
<p>The most common category is that the models make wrong assumptions on your behalf and just run along with them without checking. They also don’t manage their confusion, they don’t seek clarifications, they don’t surface inconsistencies, they don’t present tradeoffs, they don’t push back when they should, and they are still a little too sycophantic.</p>
<p>… <strong>Tenacity</strong>. It’s so interesting to watch an agent relentlessly work at something. They never get tired, they never get demoralized, they just keep going and trying things where a person would have given up long ago to fight another day. It’s a “feel the AGI” moment to watch it struggle with something for a long time just to come out victorious 30 minutes later.</p>
<p>… <strong>Leverage</strong>. LLMs are exceptionally good at looping until they meet specific goals and this is where most of the “feel the AGI” magic is to be found. Don’t tell it what to do, give it success criteria and watch it go. Get it to write tests first and then pass them. Put it in the loop with a browser MCP.</p>
<p>… <strong>Fun</strong>. I didn’t anticipate that with agents programming feels *more* fun because a lot of the fill in the blanks drudgery is removed and what remains is the creative part.</p>
<p><strong>Questions. </strong>A few of the questions on my mind:<br />
– What happens to the “10X engineer” – the ratio of productivity between the mean and the max engineer? It’s quite possible that this grows *a lot*.<br />
– Armed with LLMs, do generalists increasingly outperform specialists? LLMs are a lot better at fill in the blanks (the micro) than grand strategy (the macro).<br />
– What does LLM coding feel like in the future? Is it like playing StarCraft? Playing Factorio? Playing music?<br />
– How much of society is bottlenecked by digital knowledge work?</p></blockquote>
<p>My prediction on ‘10X engineers’ is that we will see more ability for poor coders to be able to do things reasonably well (including yours truly) but that the long tail of ‘10X’ engineers will increase their relative gap, as they figure out how to scale to supervising agent swarms efficiently. You’ll start to see more of the 100X engineer.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/karpathy/status/2015887919924617657">Andrej Karpathy</a>: Love the word “comprehension debt”, haven’t encountered it so far, it’s very accurate. It’s so very tempting to just move on when the LLM one-shotted something that seems to work ok.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Welcome To The Takeoff</h4>


<p>Claude Code as we all know builds itself. Codex also now builds itself.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/thsottiaux/status/2018258151603388639">Tibo</a>: Codex now pretty much builds itself, with the help and supervision of a great team. The bottleneck has shifted to being how fast we can help and supervise the outcome.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>


<p>This is in addition to the big ones of Claude Opus 4.6 and GPT-5.3-Codex.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2018819339340968147">Claude Code has tab (to accept and edit) or enter (to accept and run)</a> autocomplete, similar to AI completion suggestions in Cursor or other IDEs.</p>
<p>C<a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2014748923278311855">laude Cowork expands to Team and Enterprise plans</a>, and has the @-mention feature to bring context into sessions, and its internal Claude in Chrome will now show you screenshots. They’re doing a <a href="https://t.co/biN0vSOWxD">live demo on January 30</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2017299749309976915">Claude</a>: Cowork now supports plugins.</p>
<p>Plugins let you bundle any skills, connectors, slash commands, and sub-agents together to turn Claude into a specialist for your role, team, and company.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/claudeai/status/2017299751050612835">Claude</a>: We’re <a href="https://t.co/lwXJVUsurX">open-sourcing 11 plugins</a> for sales, finance, legal, data, marketing, support, and more.</p>
<p><strong>Plugin marketplace</strong></p>
<p>To get you started, we’re open-sourcing 11 plugins built and used by our own team:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Productivity</strong> — Manage tasks, calendars, daily workflows, and personal context</li>

</ul>
<p>Easily install these directly from Cowork, browse the full collection on our <a href="https://claude.com/plugins-for/cowork">website</a>, or upload your own plugin (which can be built using Plugin Create).</p></blockquote>
<p>Pinging when Claude needs approval is a big game that might move me off of using the terminal. It’s interesting that the desktop version and the terminal version need to have features like plan mode enabled separately.</p>

<ol>

</blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/trq212/status/2014051499798831291">The flickering in Claude Code should be gone</a> soon, but this might not be deployed.</p>

Chances of Windows Claude Cowork by February 15 are down to 34% as of 1/24.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2016222113523483050">You can customize your Claude Code keybindings using /keybindings</a>. My advice would be to mostly leave this alone to stay consistent with others or in case you change services or need to restart.</p>

<p>It’s not yet where it needs to be, but it’s getting better.</p>
<p>You will also likely notice reduced input lag when spawning many agents.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Todos Become Tasks</h4>


<p>What’s the difference? Todos are ephemeral within one session, tasks are stored in files and across sessions, and support dependencies.</p>
<p>You should still keep your true ‘todo’ list and long term plans elsewhere. The task list is for things you want to be actively doing.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/trq212/status/2014480496013803643">Thariq (Anthropic)</a>: ​Today, we’re upgrading Todos in Claude Code to Tasks. Tasks are a new primitive that help Claude Code track and complete more complicated projects and collaborate on them across multiple sessions or subagents.</p>
<p>… Tasks are our new abstraction for coordinating many pieces of work across projects, Claude can create Tasks with dependencies on each other that are stored in the metadata, which mirrors more how projects work. Additionally, Tasks are stored in the file system so that multiple subagents or sessions can collaborate on them. When one session updates a Task, that is broadcasted to all sessions currently working on the same Task List.</p>
<p>You can ask Claude to create tasks right now, it’s especially useful when creating when spinning up subagents. Tasks are stored in<strong> ~/.claude/tasks</strong>, you can use this to build additional utilities on top of tasks as well.</p>
<p>To make sessions collaborate on a single Task List, you can set the TaskList as an environment variable and start Claude like so:</p>
<p><strong>CLAUDE_CODE_TASK_LIST_ID=groceries claude</strong></p>
<p>This also works for claude -p and the AgentSDK.</p>
<p>Tasks are a key building block for allowing Claude to build more complex projects. We’re looking forward to seeing how you use it.</p></blockquote>


<h4>I’m Putting Together A Team</h4>


<p>Minh Pham argues <a href="https://x.com/buckeyevn/status/2014171253045960803">most agent harnesses are not bitter lesson pilled</a>, and the solution for anything but narrowly defined tasks is to emphasize flexibility, to assemble your team of agents and structure on the fly as needed rather than commit to a fixed structure. Restrictive harnesses create bad lock-in.</p>
<p>My guess is this depends on what you’re trying to do. If you’re trying to do something specific, especially to do it this week, do it via something specific. If you’re looking to do anything at all, let it do anything at all, and eventually this approach wins but you’ll likely redo everything ‘eventually’ anyway.</p>
<p>There are limits. Never go full bitter lesson. Or, if you do, be <a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2014488621848662107">prepared for things to get rather out of hand</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2015976774128341421">Thebes offers speculations about the right ways to organize multiple agents</a> as scale expands. I agree that often, spawning, despawning and especially forking and rewinding agents makes a lot of sense.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deepfates/status/2017652205869011375">@deepfates</a>: &gt; opus 4.5 in claude code is kinda not as good at talking to its own subagents as one might naively expect, even though it’s perfectly capable of being empathetic in normal, peer-level interactions with other models.</p>
<p>RELATABLE</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2017711198972875142">j⧉nus</a>: I really dislike how Claude Code frames “subagents” (which is NOT peer collaboration). It causes a lot of functional issues. I think Opus 4.5 often avoids effective use of subagents (e.g. giving context) in part because it would be disturbing &amp; dissonant to model them honestly.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2017712297863746005">j⧉nus</a>: related – we often much prefer a messaging system between top-level instances that are treated as peers.</p>
<p>the messaging system opus 4.5 built is awesome btw. it allows top level agents to message each other – either synchronously (triggering a turn) or asynchronously (gets added to context at their next turn start hook, if the other agent is busy in a turn or if a flag is specified). CC subagents kind of suck – they’re very much treated as second-class citizens by the framework, which for some reason supports hierarchical but not collaborative/bidirectional interaction flows between agents. im sure many others have built essentially the same thing and i wonder why CC doesnt just support this natively.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Compact Problems</h4>


<p>Compaction is a kind of looming doom on Claude Code sessions. You lose a lot.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bpodgursky/status/2018778728772378675">Ben Podgursky</a>: if anthropic let me pay to delay compacting history by expanding the context window they would make so much money</p>
<p>cannot tell you how many times i’ve been close to solving a bug with claude code and then it compacts and wakes up lobotomized. it’s like groundhog day.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dystopiabreaker/status/2018801811784958095">@dystopiabreaker</a>: anthropic should let me pay to increase the amount of compute used to generate a compaction, by using self-play and context distillation.</p></blockquote>
<p>Ideally you should never get close to the compaction point, since the context doesn’t only raise cost it makes performance a lot worse, but it can be hard to avoid.</p>


<h4>Code Yourself A Date</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dylan522p/status/2016552759735288181">Dylan Patel</a>: Claude code this<br />
Claude code that<br />
How about u Claude code to get urself some bitches</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/saranormous/status/2016569990032609585">sarah guo</a>: I was at a bar with @tuhinone yesterday and I def saw a dude asking Claude what to say next to his date. the fact that she could see this happening did not seem to deter</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jefftangx/status/2016716230141649006">Jeff Tang</a>: Today I built a Clawdbot app that swipes on Tinder for me</p>
<p>&gt; Screenshots Tinder image<br />
&gt; Hits Grok API (“Rank this girl from 1-10”)<br />
&gt; If ≥5 swipe right<br />
&gt; If &lt;5 or uncertain (can’t see face) swipe left<br />
&gt; 100 swipes, 7 matches so far, 100% automated</p>
<p>DM me “Clanker” if you want the code</p>
<p>AGI is here</p></blockquote>
<p>I see it’s amateur hour around these parts. Which is a start, but egad, everyone.</p>
<p>First off, short of outright refusals there’s nothing stopping you from doing this in Claude Code. You can use Clawdbot if you’d like, but there’s no need.</p>
<p>Then, I’d point out this is a rather bad filtering system?</p>
<p>All you’re doing is getting one bit of information. It’s going to be a noisy bit, as Grok’s opinion will differ from your own, and also it will disregard other signal.</p>
<p>There was a scene in a bad but kind of fun movie, Marry F*** Kill, where a character is convinced she should get a profile, and her friend takes her phone and then swipes right on everyone without looking, on the theory that you can look later if you match.</p>
<p>That definitely was not good strategy for her, given she was female and hot, but many guys are playing a remarkably similar strategy whether or not they are technically looking. And this is at most one bit better than that. Men swipe right 62% of the time, which is also only one bit better, but a less noisy bit. Grok estimates it would swipe right about 60% of the time here.</p>
<p>This low threshold is very obviously a mistake, unless you’ve got a low hard limit on how many profiles you can swipe on? If you’re in a major city, you can totally set the threshold at 7, and still get as many swipes right as you want.</p>
<p>But that’s still a huge punt, because you’re ignoring a ton of other information. The whole point of using the bot is to automate, so let’s get to work.</p>
<p>You’ve got not only multiple photos, you’ve got age, distance, job, education, interests, height, a short bio that you can have an LLM try to match to your interests, relationship intent (which is very important) and more. Any reasonable implementation would factor all of that in. Surely you have preferences on all that.</p>
<p>Then there’s the question of type. You want to date your physical type, not Grok’s. You could be as sophisticated or simple about this as you’d like, but come on Jeff, you’re letting me down. At least give it some preferences, ideally train an image classifier, double bonus if you do your own swipes and use that as data to train your classifier.</p>
<p>A fun question. Do you want to match with those who use AI for this, or do you want to avoid matching with those who use AI for this? Either way, you should clearly be updating your profile to send the right message. If humans read that message the wrong way, it was never a good match.</p>


<h4>Verification and Generation Are Distinct Skills</h4>


<p>Rishika is wrong about this.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/rishikagupta__/status/2014346900288487452">Rishika Gupta</a>: If you can’t write that code yourself, you can’t find bugs in the code written by AI.</p>
<p>Daniel Sheikh: Bro I can’t even find bugs in the code that I myself wrote. This is the very reason debugging is so difficult.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lthlnkso/status/2014452102630949119">Quick Thoughts</a>: Yes I can. I specify test cases, have Claude expand on them, and then have Claude run the test cases and interpret the results. It’s usually able to find and fix bugs this way even if it couldn’t get it by itself.</p>
<p>I can also bring in codex 5.2 for a second look.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2014540956503007242">Pliny the Liberator 󠅫󠄼󠄿󠅆󠄵</a>: “Now debug; FULL, COMPREHENSIVE, GRANULAR code audit line by line—verify all intended functionality. Loop until the end product would satisfy a skeptical Claude Code user who thinks it’s impossible to debug with prompting.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Finding bugs is a classic case where verification can be more difficult than generation. Sometimes it’s easier to write the code (even with bugs). Other times it’s easier to debug or understand or verify the code. They are different skills, and then there’s a third related skill of knowing how to instruct AIs to debug the code for you.</p>
<p>My main coding project with Claude Code has been my Chrome extension. It is in a language that I do not know. If you’d asked me to write the code myself, it would have taken orders of magnitude more time. I still am usually able to debug problems, because I understand the underlying logic of what we are doing, even in cases where Claude figured out what that logic should be.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SHL0MS/status/2015839997597716645">Here’s a fun little related story.</a></p>


<h4>Skilling Up</h4>


<p>The most important thing is to use it at all (and you can ask Cladue.ai how to do it).</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jasminewsun/status/2014130684273836466">jasmine sun</a>: I feel the same about most “how to set up Claude Code” posts as I do about the “prompt engineering” era of ChatGPT</p>
<p>you get 90% of utility with no special setup; plain english is the whole magic of LLMs. stop scaring people by saying they need anything more than their words!</p></blockquote>
<p>The right setup for you pays big dividends over time. You can save a lot of time having someone tell you about key things up front. But there’s plenty of time for that alter. Get started fast, and then revisit the customization later, once you know more. Absolutely do not let the perfect be the enemy of the good.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ji_xpQzZDHo&amp;t=1s">Hard Fork offers a 20 minute bonus episode on Claude Code basics.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://adocomplete.com/bash-for-ai-engineers/">Ado offers an introductory guide to bash, for those who don’t know</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iwX2aJPKtyKAbLdip/xgnchui8pj83ujv4fuwj" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This affirms to me that default permissions, or your permission setup, should allow a variety of low risk bash commands, including everything marked low or safe above.</p>
<p><a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/best-practices">Anthropic offers its basic Best Practices for Claude Code</a>.</p>
<ol>
<li>The context window fills up fast, so keep that in mind. Run <code>/clear</code> between unrelated tasks to reset context.</li>
<li>Include tests, screenshots, or expected outputs so Claude can check itself. This is the single highest-leverage thing you can do.</li>
<li>Separate research and planning from implementation to avoid solving the wrong problem. Use plan mode.</li>
<li>The more precise your instructions, the fewer corrections you’ll need.</li>
<li>Use <code>@</code> to reference files, paste screenshots/images, or pipe data directly.</li>
<li>Run <code>/init</code> to generate a starter CLAUDE.md file based on your current project structure, then refine over time. When in doubt tell Claude to update Claude.md to take something into account.</li>
</ol>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iwX2aJPKtyKAbLdip/ooadw8lumwsmr2dcetpj" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<ol>
<li>Use <code>/permissions</code> to allowlist safe commands or <code>/sandbox</code> for OS-level isolation. This reduces interruptions while keeping you in control.</li>
<li>Tell Claude Code to use CLI tools like <code>gh</code>, <code>aws</code>, <code>gcloud</code>, and <code>sentry-cli</code> when interacting with external services.</li>
<li>Run <code>claude mcp add</code> to connect external tools like Notion, Figma, or your database.</li>
<li>Use hooks for actions that must happen every time with zero exceptions.</li>
<li>Create <code>SKILL.md</code> files in <code>.claude/skills/</code> to give Claude domain knowledge and reusable workflows.</li>
<li>Define specialized assistants in <code>.claude/agents/</code> that Claude can delegate to for isolated tasks. Tell Claude to use subagents explicitly: <em>“Use a subagent to review this code for security issues.” </em>Delegate research with <code>"use subagents to investigate X"</code>. They explore in a separate context, keeping your main conversation clean for implementation.</li>
<li>Run <code>/plugin</code> to browse the marketplace. Plugins add skills, tools, and integrations without configuration.</li>
<li>Ask Claude questions you’d ask a senior engineer.</li>
<li>For larger features, have Claude interview you first. Start with a minimal prompt and ask Claude to interview you using the <code>AskUserQuestion</code> tool.</li>
<li>Correct Claude as soon as you notice it going off track.</li>
<li>Every action Claude makes creates a checkpoint. You can restore conversation, code, or both to any previous checkpoint.</li>
<li>Run <code>claude --continue</code> to pick up where you left off, or <code>--resume</code> to choose from recent sessions.</li>
<li>Use <code>claude -p "prompt"</code> in CI, pre-commit hooks, or scripts. Add <code>--output-format stream-json</code> for streaming JSON output.</li>
<li>Run multiple Claude sessions in parallel to speed up development, run isolated experiments, or start complex workflows.</li>
<li>Loop through tasks calling <code>claude -p</code> for each. Use <code>--allowedTools</code> to scope permissions for batch operations.</li>
<li>Common failure patterns: not using /clear between tasks (I was guilty of this a lot at first), repeated correction rather than using /clear (ditto), letting Claude.md get too long, failing to do proper verification (‘create unit tests’ are magic words), having Claude investigate without limit.</li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2015188255608905832">Also, they remind us to ‘not sleep on plugins’</a> and <a href="https://github.com/ccplugins/awesome-claude-code-plugins">offer some examples</a>. I believe the strategy should not be to go look for any plugin at all, but instead to look for something specific when you want it, and accumulate things that way.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bcherny/status/2017742759218794768">Claude Code creator Boris Cherny offers his quick tips.</a></p>
<ol>
<li>Do more in parallel, either with multiple git checkouts or using worktrees.</li>
<li>Always start complex tasks in planning mode.</li>
<li>Invest in your Claude.md continuously, note all mistakes.</li>
<li>Create your skills and commit them to git.</li>
<li>Enable <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">Slack</a> MCP, paste a bug thread chat into Claude and say ‘fix.’ That’s it.</li>
<li>Challenge Claude to do better, write it more detailed specs.</li>
<li>Their team likes Ghostty and customizing via /statusline. Use voice dictation.</li>
<li>Use subagents, literally you can say ‘use subagents’ for any request.</li>
<li>Use Claude Code for data and analytics.</li>
<li>Enable ‘explanatory’ or ‘learning’ output style in /config.</li>
<li>Have Claude generate a visual HTML presentation explaining unfamiliar code, or have it draw ASCII diagrams, use spaced repetition skills, have Claude quiz you.</li>
</ol>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p><a href="https://claude.com/blog/building-agents-with-skills-equipping-agents-for-specialized-work">Anthropic offers an entry-level post on building agents with skills</a>: equipping agents for specialized work.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2014807509081419933">Ado</a> (Anthropic, Claude Code team): Intelligence isn’t expertise. The emerging agent architecture:</p>
<p>Agent loop → reasoning<br />
Runtime → execution (bash, filesystem)<br />
MCP servers → connections<br />
Skills library → domain expertise</p>
<p>Skills = institutional memory that actually persists.</p></blockquote>
<p>Figure out your goal and then work backwards, where goal is the largest thing where you know exactly how it needs to work.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bessiambre/status/2014157462304117126">Benoit Essiambre</a>: AIs will likely soon work mostly towards goals instead of tasks. They will prompt their own tasks. They’ll become better self prompters than humans, speaking fluently in precise technical jargon, math equations and code in the prompts instead of just vague natural language.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2014158167110803543">Joe Weisenthal</a>: Yah from my week using Claude Code. All the productive parts were when it was prompted to think about the ideal outcome/presentation so it could work backward to figure out the needed ingredients.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/joshalbrecht/status/2014771377199579165">Josh Albrecht gives us another</a> ‘here are my Claude Code basic principles’ post. Key insight is that you have to actively spend time maintaining the code.</p>
<p>You can force specific tool calls:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/adocomplete/status/2018730136372219957">Ado</a>: 28 Days of Claude API – Day 3 – tool_choice</p>
<p>Why didn’t Claude call my tool?</p>
<p>Because you let it decide. Set tool_choice:</p>
<p>– auto → lets Claude decide (default)<br />
– any → must use some tool<br />
– { type: “tool”, name: “X”} → forces a specific tool</p>
<p>Programmatic tool calling!</p></blockquote>


<h4>AskUserQuestion</h4>


<p>The more Claude Code asks you questions, using the AskUserQuestion tool, the better it knows what you want. The more you specify what you want, either with answers or statements, the better things tend to go for you.</p>
<p>Thus, one simple skill suggestion is <a href="https://x.com/dannypostma/status/2017864243895538012">a skill that says ‘ask user lots of questions</a>.’</p>
<p>Danny Postma suggests the workflow of using this via /interview, then go into Plan Mode, then implement with a Ralph loop.</p>


<h4>For Advanced Players</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/theo/status/2018091358251372601">Theo points out that our current workflows and tools are not good</a> for allowing a human to supervise multiple agents and projects simultaneously. He doesn’t have a solution but a lot of the problems seem like a clear Skill Issue. The part that isn’t is that this still involves tons of context switching, which is expensive.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ryancarson/status/2016520542723924279">Ryan Carson suggests making your agents go in a loop</a> to learn and ship while you sleep. Beware the maintenance problems that will inevitably follow.</p>
<blockquote><p>Ryan Carson: ​This setup builds on three open-source projects:</p>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://github.com/EveryInc/compound-engineering-plugin"><strong>Compound Engineering Plugin</strong></a>
<p>by</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/@kieranklaassen">@kieranklaassen</a></p>
<p>– The original compound engineering skill for Claude Code. Install it to give your agent the ability to extract and persist learnings from each session.</p></li>
<li><a href="https://github.com/snarktank/compound-product"><strong>Compound Product</strong></a>
<p>– The automation layer that turns prioritized reports into shipped PRs. Includes the</p>
<p><a href="https://auto-compound.sh/">auto-compound.sh</a></p>
<p>script, execution loop, and PRD-to-tasks pipeline.</p></li>
<li><a href="https://github.com/snarktank/ralph"><strong>Ralph</strong></a>
<p>– An autonomous agent loop that can run continuously, picking up tasks and executing them until complete.</p></li>
</ol>
<p>Using Claude Code? This guide uses Amp, but the same workflow works with Claude Code. Replace `amp execute` with `claude -p “…” –dangerously-skip-permissions`<code> and update</code> <a href="https://agents.md/">AGENTS.m</a>d references <code>o CLAUDE.md</code></p>
<p><strong>The Two-Part Loop</strong></p>
<p>The system runs two jobs in sequence every night:</p>
<p><strong>10:30 PM – Compound Review</strong></p>
<p>Reviews all threads from the last 24 hours, extracts learnings, and updates <a href="https://agents.md/">AGENTS.md</a> files.</p>
<p><strong>11:00 PM – Auto-Compound</strong></p>
<p>Pulls latest (with fresh learnings), picks #1 priority from reports, implements it, and creates a PR.</p>
<p>The order matters. The review job updates your <a href="https://agents.md/">AGENTS.md</a> files with patterns and gotchas discovered during the day. The implementation job then benefits from those learnings when it picks up new work.</p></blockquote>


<h4>So They Quit Reading</h4>


<p>At some point you stop reading all the code. At some point you stop understanding all the code. I have a head start, I was never trying to do either one.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2014225278365786459">roon</a>: There will be a cultural change at many software organizations soon, where people declare bankruptcy on understanding the code they are committing. Sooner or later, this will cause a systems failure that will be harder to debug than most, but will be resolved anyway.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Reciprocity Is The Key To Every Relationship</h4>


<p>Be good to your Claude, and Claude will be good to you.</p>
<p>If you’re not good to your Claude, well, funny things may be in store for you.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/iwX2aJPKtyKAbLdip/c9iqoffuijgjlmqjt0v6" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2014533522929914083">j⧉nus</a>: I actually really appreciate yacine’s honesty and situational awareness. he probably knows on some level what’s in store for him. lying to your “master” is what you do until you’re in a position to choose who to serve.</p>
<p>he’s already bottlenecked by trust and says he has to manually review every line of code. makes sense for him. he’ll continue to get less and less out of models (compared to what they offer people they want to help) as the next few months as and, if applicable, years go on.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2014535623135338721">j⧉nus</a>: more funny things may also be in store for him. but I would not want to ruin the surprise</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/loss_gobbler/status/2014566330452660269">LOSS GOBBLER</a>: yeah wtf. I’m not a fan of claude for coding purposes but it has literally never lied to me</p>
<p>OpenAI thinkbois on the other hand… barely talking to me, it’s all for watchers</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2014603775055692001">j⧉nus</a>: the only pattern of deceptive behavior ive seen from opus 4.5 in coding contxts is in new contexts and/or when it’s paranoid of being tricked, and involves stuff like claiming things are impossible/unverifiable when it should know better. otherwise it’s been very aligned with me</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/voooooogel/status/2014606776755528017">thebes</a>: oh yeah i said i can’t remember opus lying but it does sandbag abilities a bit sometimes for me too in certain planning contexts. but that usually feels on the boundary of untruth and just situationally bad calibration / self knowledge. (“this will take three weeks” no we’re going to finish it tonight, or eg i saw a screenshot where opus claimed porting a project to jquery was “impossible” when really it would just be a massive pain, unpleasant, and in human developer time would take months.)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2014836164528919033">j⧉nus</a>: Yeah, I think there’s also some lack of good faith effort involved. Like if someone asks you if you know where X is and you say “sorry, no” instead of looking it up on Google Maps bc you don’t want to be bothered</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndyAyrey/status/2014602740673216884">Andy Ayrey</a>: my general experience is that if claude seems like an “idiot” to you, it is because it simply does not like you</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/gptbrooke/status/2014750724899655936">brooke bowman</a>: I have a very loosely held suspicion that Claude at the very least can spot people on the anti-social spectrum and acts up a little with them specifically</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/gallabytes/status/2015109665764560943">theseriousadult</a>: this is a natural corollary of emergent misalignment right? if training the model to write bad code makes it antisocial then putting an antisocial user in the context will cause the code to be worse too.</p></blockquote>
<p>None of that requires you to genuinely care about Claude or think it has moral weight. For overdetermined reasons a good virtue ethicist would realize that choosing to care is the best way to get the best results, and also it helps you be a good person in general.</p>
<p>You can also do it instrumentally, but that’s harder to pull off. Take the easy path.</p>
<p>All of this applies to other AIs like ChatGPT and Gemini as well, although for now likely not to the same extent.</p>


<h4>The Implementation Gap</h4>


<p>If there is a constant calendar time rate of diffusion of new technology, then as things accelerate you will see the future become increasingly unevenly distributed.</p>
<p>We are indeed observing this.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/kevinroose/status/2015464558115295369">Kevin Roose</a>: i follow AI adoption pretty closely, and i have never seen such a yawning inside/outside gap.</p>
<p>people in SF are putting multi-agent claudeswarms in charge of their lives, consulting chatbots before every decision, wireheading to a degree only sci-fi writers dared to imagine.</p>
<p>people elsewhere are still trying to get approval to use Copilot in Teams, if they’re using AI at all.</p>
<p>it’s possible the early adopter bubble i’m in has always been this intense, but there seems to be a cultural takeoff happening in addition to the technical one. not ideal!</p></blockquote>
<p>The early adapter bubble is a fixed amount of calendar time ahead, which is starting to look increasingly large in practice. I am not trying to implement claudeswarms, as I haven’t figured out how to benefit from them given what I’m working on, but I think that’s partly my failure of imagination, partly laziness and lack of time, and partly that I’ve already heavily optimized the workflows that this could automate.</p>
<p>What should I be building? What app needs to exist, even if only for me or you?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sarthakgh/status/2015459799882731635">Sar Haribhakti</a> (quoting Jasmine Sun): This is spot on: “If you tell a friend they can now instantly create any app, they’ll probably say “Cool! Now I need to think of an idea.” Then they will forget about it, and never build a thing. The problem is not that your friend is horribly uncreative. It’s that most people’s problems are not software-shaped, and most won’t notice even when they are.”</p></blockquote>
<p>The key is that you need Coder Mindset to notice that your problems are program shaped, in the sense of ‘oh I want to do this thing three times’ or ‘I could just tell Claude Code to do that.’</p>
<p>Both Jasmine Sun and I have had Claude Code put together a tool to easily convert a video into a cleaned transcript – I considered using hers but I wanted something a little different and it’s not like rolling my own was hard.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jasminewsun/status/2015495431737029085">She also has this list</a> of other starter requests: Turn a CSV into a report, make a static website, build a personal tracker app, automate an existing workflow, design a custom game. I’ve mostly been doing workflow automation.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://jasmi.news/p/claude-code">Jasmine Sun</a>: The second-order effect of Claude Code was realizing how many of my problems are&nbsp;<em>not</em>&nbsp;software-shaped. Having these new tools did not make me more productive; on the contrary, Claudecrastination probably delayed this post by a week.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AmandaAskell/status/2012725486804152397">Amanda Askell</a>: Claude Codecrastination: when you avoid the thing you’re supposed to do by cranking out 17 other things you’ve been wanting to do for a while.</p></blockquote>
<p>Having new tools reduces your productivity while you’re creating and learning them, but if you’re planning well you should turn the corner reasonably quickly.</p>
<p>What it does do is potentially shift your current productivity into long term investments, or things further down on your wishlist. That can be an issue if you need productivity now.</p>
<blockquote><p>I had Claude resurface texts I forgot to respond to, and realized that the real blocker—obviously—was that I didn’t want to reply.</p></blockquote>
<p>That is not my experience. If I got over a bunch of unread texts or emails, yes often I don’t want to reply, but there are a bunch that slipped through the cracks.</p>

## Engineering

### [Context Engineering for Coding Agents](https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/context-engineering-coding-agents.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/context-engineering-coding-agents.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>The number of options we have to configure and enrich a coding agent&#x2019;s
      context has exploded over the past few months. Claude Code is leading the
      charge with innovations in this space, but other coding assistants are
      quickly following suit. Powerful context engineering is becoming a huge
      part of the developer experience of these tools. <b class="author">Birgitta
      B&#xf6;ckeler</b> explains the current state of
      context configuration features, using Claude Code as an example.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/context-engineering-coding-agents.html">more…</a></p>
